<!-- head-1: body_md -->
Throughout the paper, $n$ and $m$ denote the two sample sizes, and we
assume without loss of generality that $1\le n\le m$. We write $A\lesssim B$ if $A\le CB$ for some constant $C>0$, and $A\gtrsim B$ if $B\lesssim A$. We write $A\asymp B$ when both $A\lesssim B$ and
$A\gtrsim B$ hold. Subscripts indicate the parameters on which the implicit constants may depend; for example, $A\lesssim_{p,r}B$ means that the corresponding constant depends only on $p$ and $r$. We also use $C_p$, $C_{p,r}$, and similar notation for positive constants depending only on the indicated parameters. Their values may change from line to line. For $x\in\mathbb R$, we write $[x]_+ \coloneqq \max\{x,0\}$ for its positive part and $\lceil x\rceil$ for the ceiling of $x$. Finally, we denote by $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ the unit sphere in $\mathbb{R}^d$, by $\sigma$ the uniform probability measure on $\mathbb{S}^{d-1}$. For a probability measure $\xi$ on $\mathbb{R}^d$, a number $q\ge 1$, and $\theta \in \mathbb{S}^{d-1}$, we denote by $M_{\xi,r}(\theta)\coloneqq \mathbb{E}_{X \sim \xi}\left|\langle X,\theta \rangle\right|^r$.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:PropertiesK_pr}
Let $r\geq p\geq 1$ and $b>0$. Then, $ \mathcal K_{p,r}(b)\subseteq\mathcal P_p(\mathbb R^d).$
Moreover, for every $B,R>0$,
$$ \left\{ \mu\in\mathcal P_r(\mathbb R^d): \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\left[\|X\|^r\right]\le B
\right\}\subseteq
\mathcal K_{p,r}\left(B^{p/r}\right),
$$
and
$$
\left\{\mu\in\mathcal P(\mathbb R^d):
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\mathbb B(0,R)\right\}\subseteq
\mathcal K_{p,r}(R^p),
$$
where $\mathbb B(0,R)$ denotes the closed Euclidean ball of radius $R$ centered at the origin. 

Finally, for every $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$, $\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu) \le 2^p b.$
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:minimax-noncritical}
Let $p\ge1$, $r> p$, $b>0$. Suppose that $r\neq 2p$. The plug-in estimator is minimax rate-optimal over
$\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$. More precisely,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\mathrm{plug}}
\left(\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)
\right) \asymp_{p,r} \mathcal R_{n,m}^{\star} \left( \mathcal K_{p,r}(b)
\right)\asymp_{p,r}b
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
n^{-(r-p)/r}, & p< r<2p,\\[2mm]
n^{-1/2}, & r>2p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:distinct-distributions}
The lower bound in the regime $p<r<2p$ remains valid when the
parameter space is restricted to pairs of distinct distributions. Indeed, the testing pairs constructed in Subsection~\ref{sec:lowerboundp<r<2p} satisfy $\mu_0\neq\nu_0$ and $\mu_1\neq\nu_1$. Consequently,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\inf_{\widehat T}
\sup_{\substack{\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)\\\mu\neq\nu
}}\mathbb E_{\mu,\nu}\left|
\widehat T-\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)
\right|\asymp_{p,r}bn^{-(r-p)/r}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus, the same minimax rate persists even when the parameter space is
restricted to pairs of distinct distributions, confirming the
expectation stated by \citet{manole2022minimax} that excluding the
diagonal $\mu=\nu$ does not restore the parametric rate.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:minimal-moment-order}
The construction used to establish the minimax lower bound in the
regime $p<r<2p$ remains valid at the endpoint $r=p$. Consequently,
$$
\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\star} \left( \mathcal K_{p,p}(b) \right) \gtrsim_p b.
$$
Thus, the minimax risk does not vanish as the sample sizes increase,
and no estimator is uniformly consistent over
$\mathcal K_{p,p}(b)$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->

Let $p\ge1$, $b>0$. Then

\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\star}

<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:projected-empirical-rate}
Let $q\ge 1$ and $s>q$. Let $Q$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb{R}^d$ and $\widehat{Q}_n$ be the empirical measure associated with $n$ i.i.d. samples from $Q$. Fix $\theta\in \mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ such that
$M_{Q,s}(\theta)<\infty$. Then,
$$
\mathbb E\operatorname{W}_q^q\left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right)\lesssim_{q,s}\left(M_{Q,s}(\theta)\right)^{q/s}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
n^{-(s-q)/s}, & q<s<2q,\\
\log (n+1)n^{-1/2}, &s=2q,\\
n^{-1/2}, & s>2q.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:decomposition}
Let $p>1$. For the fixed $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and
$\theta\in\mathcal G$, where $\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and $\mathcal G$ are defined in Eqs.~\eqref{sec1:eqclass} and
\eqref{eq:gooddirections}, respectively, we have
                                                                                    
                                                               
                                                                             
                                                    

                                                                                                                      
                                                                          
                                                                          
                                                                          
                                                                             
                                                        
                                                   
    
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\nu}_m\right)-\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu\right)\right| \lesssim_p\left(\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}+\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}\right),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
where
$$\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta} \coloneqq \mathbb{E}\int_0^1\left(|F_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}+
|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\right)\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du,
$$
$$\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\int_0^1\left(|F_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}+|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\right)\left|G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du,
$$
and
$$\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\int_0^1\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^pdu,\qquad \mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\int_0^1\left|G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^pdu.
$$
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:peelingtool}
Let $r>2$. For the fixed $\mu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and
$\theta\in\mathcal G$, we have, for every $0\le \alpha\le1$,
                                                                                                                                                                                                                 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    
                                                                                           
   
                                    
$$
\mathbb E\int_0^\alpha\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du \lesssim_r \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The case $\alpha=0$ is trivial. We may therefore assume that
$0<\alpha\le1$. Adapting the arguments used in \citet[Proposition~2.17]{santambrogio2015optimal} to the interval
$(0,\alpha)$ yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E \int_0^\alpha \left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|du = \int_{\mathbb R}
\mathbb E\left| \min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\} - \min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}
\right|dx,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

When $\alpha\ge1/4$, the desired estimate follows directly from the
global empirical measure estimation bound in $\operatorname{W}_1$. We may therefore assume
that $0<\alpha<1/4$ and decompose $\mathbb R$ into
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
J_1\coloneqq  \left\{ x:F_\theta(x)\le2\alpha
\right\}, \qquad J_2\coloneqq \left\{
x:2\alpha<F_\theta(x)\le3/4 \right\},
\qquad J_3 \coloneqq \left\{x:F_\theta(x)>3/4\right\}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

We first derive pointwise estimates on these three regions. 

On $J_1$,
the map $t\mapsto\min\{t,\alpha\}$ is $1$-Lipschitz, and the binomial
variance bound yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E \left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\} -
\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\} \right|\le \mathbb{E}\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right| \le
\sqrt{\frac{F_\theta(x)(1-F_{\theta}(x))}{n}}\le \sqrt{\frac{F_{\theta}(x)}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

On $J_2\cup J_3$, since $F_\theta(x)>\alpha$, we have
$\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}=\alpha$, and hence
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E \left| \min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}- \min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}
\right|= \mathbb E \left[
\left(\alpha-F_{n,\theta}(x)\right)_+
\right]\le \alpha\mathbb P\left(
F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha \right).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
On $J_2$, the inequality $F_\theta(x)>2\alpha$ and a Chernoff bound
give
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb P\left( F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha\right) \le \mathbb{P}\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\frac{F_\theta(x)}{2}\right)\le\exp\left(
-\frac{nF_\theta(x)}{8}\right)\le
\exp\left(-\frac{n\alpha}{4}\right).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
On $J_3$, since $F_{\theta}(x)>\frac{3}{4}$ and $\alpha <1/4$, $F_\theta(x)-\alpha>1/2$. Then, Chebyshev's inequality
yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb P \left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha\right) \le \mathbb{P}\left(\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right| \ge F_{\theta}(x)-\alpha\right) \le \mathbb{P}\left(\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right|\ge \frac{1}{2}\right)\le
\frac{4\left(1-F_\theta(x)\right)}{n}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

To integrate these pointwise estimates, we use the following
consequence of Markov's inequality: for every $0<a<c<1$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left| \left\{x\in\mathbb R:
a<F_\theta(x)\le c\right\}\right|\le
2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}\max\left\{
a^{-1/r},(1-c)^{-1/r}\right\}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This estimate applies directly to $J_2$. On $J_1$, we further group
points according to the magnitude of $F_\theta(x)$, while on $J_3$
we group them according to the magnitude of $1-F_\theta(x)$. On each
resulting region, the corresponding pointwise estimate is uniform and
the preceding level-set bound controls its length. Since $r>2$, the
resulting geometric series are summable. Combining the contributions
of $J_1$, $J_2$, and $J_3$ yields the desired estimate.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:peelingtool}
The same estimate holds for the second sample. More precisely, for every $0\le \alpha\le 1$,
    
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
$$ \mathbb E \int_0^\alpha
\left| G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|
du\lesssim_r\frac{M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt m}\alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
Moreover, by applying the same argument to the upper tail, we obtain,
for every $0\le \beta\le1$,
$$ \mathbb E \int_{\beta}^{1}
\left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|
du \lesssim_r \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} (1-\beta)^{1/2-1/r},
$$
and similarly
$$
\mathbb E \int_\beta^1 \left| G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)- G_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|du
\lesssim_r \frac{M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt m} (1-\beta)^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:weighted-quantile}
Let $p>1$ and $r>2p$. For the fixed $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and $\theta\in\mathcal G$, where $\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and $\mathcal G$ are defined in Eqs.~\eqref{sec1:eqclass} and
\eqref{eq:gooddirections}, respectively, we have
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        
$$\mathbb E
\int_0^1 \left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right| \left| G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1} du\lesssim_{p,r}
\frac{ M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}
M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r} }{\sqrt n}.
$$
       
   
                   
           
                                                                                      
           
                                                               
   
The same bound holds with $G_\theta^{-1}$ replaced by
$F_\theta^{-1}$ and with the right-hand side replaced by
$M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}/\sqrt n$.
The analogous estimates obtained by exchanging
$(\mu,n,F_\theta,F_{n,\theta})$ and
$(\nu,m,G_\theta,G_{m,\theta})$ also hold.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
If $M_{\nu,r}(\theta)=0$, then $G_\theta^{-1}(u)=0$ for every $u\in(0,1)$. Since $p>1$, the weighted integral is equal to zero, and the result follows immediately.

We now assume that $M_{\nu,r}(\theta)\in(0,+\infty)$. For simplicity, we set
$
K\coloneqq M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}>0.\\
$
We decompose $(0,1)$ according to the size of $|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|$. Define
$$E^* \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): |G_\theta^{-1}(u)|<K
\right\},
$$
and, for $k\ge0$,
$$ E_k \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): 2^kK\le |G_\theta^{-1}(u)|<2^{k+1}K\right\}.
$$

On $E^*$, the weight is bounded by $K^{p-1}$. Hence, by Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} applied (with $Q=\mu$, $q=1, s=r$),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\int_{E^*}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du
&\le K^{p-1} \mathbb E \int_0^1
\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|du \\
&\le C_r \frac{ M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} K^{p-1}}{\sqrt n}=C_r \frac{ M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}}{\sqrt n}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

We now consider the sets $E_k$. Define
$a_k\coloneqq G_\theta(-2^kK), b_k\coloneqq G_\theta(2^kK-),
$ where
$
G_\theta(2^kK-) \coloneqq \lim_{t\to  \left(2^kK\right)^-}G_\theta(t).$ The condition $|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|\ge 2^kK$ implies
$
u\in(0,a_k]\cup[b_k,1).
$
Therefore, using Proposition~\ref{prop:peelingtool} and its upper-tail analogue, we obtain
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathbb E \int_{E_k} \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right| \left| G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1}du \\
&\le (2^{k+1}K)^{p-1} \mathbb E
\left[\int_0^{a_k} \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|du
+\int_{b_k}^1\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|du \right] \\
&\le C_r2^{(k+1)(p-1)}K^{p-1}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n}\left(a_k^{1/2-1/r}+(1-b_k)^{1/2-1/r}
\right).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$

We now bound $a_k$ and $1-b_k$. By Markov's inequality,
$$a_k =G_\theta(-2^kK)=\mathbb P_{Y\sim\nu}
\left(\langle Y,\theta\rangle\le -2^kK
\right)\le\mathbb P_{Y\sim\nu}
\left(|\langle Y,\theta\rangle|\ge 2^kK
\right)\le2^{-kr}.
$$
Similarly, since $b_k=G_\theta(2^kK-)$, we have
$$1-b_k=\mathbb P_{Y\sim\nu}\left(\langle Y,\theta\rangle\ge 2^kK\right)\le\mathbb P_{Y\sim\nu}\left(|\langle Y,\theta\rangle|\ge 2^kK\right)\le2^{-kr}.
$$
Since $r>2p>2$, we have $1/2-1/r>0$. Consequently,
$$a_k^{1/2-1/r}+(1-b_k)^{1/2-1/r}\le2^{-k(r/2-1)+1}.
$$
Hence,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:peelingcontribution}
\mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du\lesssim_{p,r}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}}{\sqrt n}2^{k(p-r/2)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

Since $r>2p$, we have $p-r/2<0$. Therefore,
$
\sum_{k\ge0}2^{k(p-r/2)}<+\infty.\\
$
Summing over $k\ge0$ and adding the contribution of $E^*$, we obtain
$$
\mathbb E\int_0^1\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|
G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du \lesssim_{p,r}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}}{\sqrt n}.
$$
The bound with $F_\theta^{-1}$ as the weight follows from the same
argument with $\nu$ replaced by $\mu$. The corresponding estimates for
the second sample follow by exchanging
$(\mu,n,F_\theta,F_{n,\theta})$ and
$(\nu,m,G_\theta,G_{m,\theta})$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
For $p=1$, the result follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} and the reverse triangle
inequality for $\operatorname W_1$. For $p>1$,
Lemma~\ref{lem:decomposition} reduces the directional error to
$\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}$, $\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}$, $\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}$, and
$\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} bounds the
unweighted terms $\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}$ and $\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}$ by $n^{-1/2}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}$ and $m^{-1/2}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}$ respectively, while Lemma~\ref{lem:weighted-quantile} gives the same
sample-size rates for the weighted terms. Integrating the resulting directional bounds over $\theta$
and applying Hölder's inequality to terms of the form $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}$ yields $\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\mathrm{plug}} \left(\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)\right)
\lesssim_{p,r}b\,n^{-1/2}.$
The complete proof is given in
Appendix~\ref{appendix:upperboundr>2p}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:truncated-weighted-quantile}
Let $p>1$ and $p<r<2p$. For the fixed $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and any $\theta\in\mathcal G$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\int_0^1
\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1}du\lesssim_{p,r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}
n^{-(r-p)/r}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The same bound holds with $G_\theta^{-1}$ replaced by
$F_\theta^{-1}$ and with the corresponding right-hand side $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}n^{-(r-p)/r}$.
Also, the analogous estimates obtained by exchanging
$(\mu,n,F_\theta,F_{n,\theta})$ and
$(\nu,m,G_\theta,G_{m,\theta})$ hold.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Set $K=M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}$. For an integer $J_n\ge1$, we
decompose $(0,1)$ into the region where
$|G_\theta^{-1}|<K$, the finitely many layers where $2^kK\le |G_\theta^{-1}|<2^{k+1}K$  for $0\le k\le J_n-1$,
and the far-tail region where
$|G_\theta^{-1}|\ge2^{J_n}K$.

On the first region, the weight is bounded, and the contribution is
controlled by the empirical measure estimation bound in $\operatorname W_1$ obtained by applying Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}. On the intermediate layers, we use Proposition~\ref{prop:peelingtool} when $r>2$ and the global empirical measure estimation bound in $\operatorname W_1$ when $1<r<2$. At $r=2$, a direct use of the critical $\operatorname W_1$ bound on every layer would introduce an additional logarithmic factor. We avoid this loss by applying Hölder's inequality and an empirical measure estimation bound in $\operatorname W_{q_0}$ for a suitably chosen $1<q_0<2/(3-p).$

The far-tail contribution is controlled directly by Markov's and
Hölder's inequalities together with the projected $r$-th moment
bounds. Choosing $J_n$ so as to balance the contributions of the
retained layers and the far-tail remainder yields the rate $n^{-(r-p)/r}$. The complete proof is given in Appendix~\ref{appendix:prooflem:truncated-weighted-quantile}.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:projected-empirical-ratev2}
Let $q\ge1$. Let $Q$ be a probability measure on $\mathbb R^d$ and
$\widehat Q_n$ be the empirical measure associated with $n$ i.i.d.\
samples from $Q$. Fix $\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$ such that
$M_{Q,2q}(\theta)<\infty$. Then
$$\mathbb E\operatorname{W}_q^q\left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right)\lesssim_q \left(M_{Q,2q}(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{\log (n+1)n^{-1}}.
$$
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:endpointweightedquantile}
Let $p>1$. For the fixed
$\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)$ and any $\theta\in\mathcal G$, we have
                                                                                                    
   
                                                 
   
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 
$$
\mathbb E\int_0^1 \left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|
\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du
\lesssim_p \left(
M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)+M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)
\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{\log (n+1)n^{-1}}.
$$
The same bound holds with $G_\theta^{-1}$ replaced by
$F_\theta^{-1}$. The analogous estimates obtained by exchanging
$(\mu,n,F_\theta,F_{n,\theta})$ and
$(\nu,m,G_\theta,G_{m,\theta})$ also hold.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Set $M_\theta
\coloneqq
M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)+M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)$. The case $M_\theta=0$ is immediate, so we assume $M_\theta>0$. 

The proof starts from the following observation. In the two preceding regimes, the peeling argument is based on the size of $\left|G^{-1}_{\theta}\right|$. More precisely, for $t>0$, Markov's inequality gives an upper bound for the Lebesgue measure of the set $\left\{u\in (0,1):\left|G^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|\ge t\right\}$ in terms of $M_{\nu,r}(\theta)$ and $t$. Thus, the measure of each region can be controlled using only the information of $G^{-1}_{\theta}$. At the critical order $r=2p$, however, replacing these measures by their Markov bounds is not sufficient. We therefore need to keep their actual values throughout the argument. The difficulty is that, when controlling the empirical quantile error, we also need to control the regions where $\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}\right|$ is large. If the decomposition is defined only through $G^{-1}_{\theta}$, the resulting layer masses do not capture the regions where $F^{-1}_{\theta}$ is large. To resolve this problem, for $u\in (0,1)$, we introduce $H_{\theta}(u) \coloneqq \max \left\{\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|, \left|G^{-1}(u)\right|\right\}.$
Then, for every $t>0$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\{H_{\theta}\ge t\} = \left\{\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}\right|\ge t\right\} \cup \left\{\left|G^{-1}_{\theta}\right|\ge t\right\},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
so the same regions can be used to control both quantiles.

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 
                   
                                                                                                                                 
                 
                                                                     
                   
                                                                                          
                 
                                                                             

We now decompose $(0,1)$ into the central region
$
E^*=\{H_\theta<M_\theta^{1/(2p)}\}
$
and the layers
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
E_k= \left\{
2^kM_\theta^{1/(2p)} \le H_\theta
<2^{k+1}M_\theta^{1/(2p)}\right\},
\qquad k\ge0.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
On $E^*$, the weight is bounded by $M_\theta^{(p-1)/(2p)}$, so the global empirical measure estimation bound in $\operatorname W_1$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} gives a contribution of order
$M_\theta^{1/2}n^{-1/2}$.

Let $\lambda$ denote the Lebesgue measure on $(0,1)$. For $k\ge0$, define
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L_k\coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1):
H_\theta(u)\ge 2^kM_\theta^{1/(2p)}
\right\}, \qquad S_k\coloneqq\lambda(L_k),
\qquad m_k\coloneqq \min\left\{S_k,\sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}}
\right\}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The key point is to retain the actual tail masses $S_k$ rather than
replace them immediately by their Markov bounds as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:weighted-quantile}. An auxiliary
localized estimate, proved in the appendix, gives
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E \int_{L_k} \left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|du\lesssim_p M_\theta^{1/(2p)}\sum_{j\ge k}2^jm_j.

Since the weight on $E_k$ is bounded by a constant multiple of

exchanging the order of summation yield
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

\sum_{k\ge0}2^{k(p-1)} \sum_{j\ge k}2^jm_j &= \sum_{j\ge0}2^jm_j\sum_{k=0}^{j}2^{k(p-1)}\lesssim_p \sum_{j\ge0}2^{jp}m_j.

        {

Thus, it remains to control
$M_\theta^{1/2}\sum_{j\ge0}2^{jp}m_j.$ By the definition of $S_j$ and Tonelli's theorem, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

\sum_{j\ge0}2^{2pj}S_j =\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{j\ge 0}2^{2pj}\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{H_{\theta}(u)\ge 2^jM_{\theta}^{1/(2p)}\right\}}du=\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{\substack{j\ge 0\\
2^j \le \frac{H_{\theta}(u)}{M_{\theta}^{1/(2p)}}}}2^{2pj}du&\lesssim_p
\frac{1}{M_\theta}\int_0^1 H_\theta(u)^{2p}du.

The definition of $H_\theta$ and the quantile-moment identity then give

\int_0^1 H_\theta(u)^{2p}du \le M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)+M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)=M_\theta.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

Hence,

\sum_{j\ge0}2^{2pj}S_j\lesssim_p1.

For any integer $J_n\ge0$, using
$m_j\le\sqrt{S_j/n}$ for $j\le J_n$, Cauchy--Schwarz and the preceding
summability estimate give

\sum_{j=0}^{J_n}2^{jp}m_j \le \frac{1}{\sqrt n}\sum_{j=0}^{J_n}\left(2^{2pj}S_j\right)^{1/2}\lesssim_p\sqrt{\frac{J_n+1}{n}}.

For the remaining layers, using the fact that $m_j\le S_j$ gives

\sum_{j>J_n}2^{jp}m_j\le 2^{-pJ_n}\sum_{j>J_n}2^{2pj}S_j\lesssim_p
2^{-pJ_n}.

Consequently,

\sum_{j\ge0}2^{jp}m_j\lesssim_p\sqrt{\frac{J_n+1}{n}}+2^{-pJ_n}.

Choosing $J_n=\left\lceil(2p)^{-1}\log_2(n+1)\right\rceil$ makes both terms bounded by a constant multiple of $\sqrt{\log(n+1)/n}.$
Combining this estimate with the contribution of $E^*$ proves the
desired weighted quantile bound. The complete proof is given in
Appendix~\ref{appendix:proofoflem:endpointweightedquantile}.

Let $\rho_\ell\coloneqq\sqrt{\log(\ell+1)/\ell}.$ When $p=1$, the result follows from
Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-ratev2} and the reverse triangle
inequality for $\operatorname W_1$.

For $p>1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:decomposition} bounds the directional error
by
$C_p(\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}+\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}).
$
Lemmas~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-ratev2} and
\ref{lem:endpointweightedquantile}, together with
$\sqrt{u+v}\le\sqrt u+\sqrt v,
$
therefore yield
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left|
\operatorname W_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat\mu_n,
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat\nu_m\right)
-\operatorname W_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu\right)\right|\lesssim_p
\left[M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)^{1/2}+
M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)^{1/2}\right](\rho_n+\rho_m).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $n\le m$ and $\ell\mapsto\log(\ell+1)/\ell$ is decreasing,
$\rho_m\le\rho_n$. Integrating over $\mathcal G$ and using
$\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)$, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\mathrm{plug}}
\left(\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)\right)
\lesssim_p\rho_n\int_{\mathcal{G}}\left[M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)^{1/2}+M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)^{1/2}
\right]d\sigma(\theta)\lesssim_p
b\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This proves the desired upper bound.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:extendedlemma3.8ledoux}
Let $0<s \le \frac{1}{2}$ and $n\le m$ such that $ns \ge 1$. Let $\widetilde N\sim \text{Binomial}(n,s)$ and $\widetilde M\sim \text{Binomial}(m,s)$
be independent. Then there exists a universal constant $c>0$ such that
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left|\frac{\widetilde N}{n}-\frac{\widetilde M}{m} \right| \ge c\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:onedimensionalcaser=2p}
Let $p\ge 1$ and $B>0$. Assume that $1 \le n \le m$. Then there exists a probability measure $P$ on $\mathbb{R}$ such that
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}} \left|x\right|^{2p}dP(x)\right)^{1/2} \le B.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Moreover, if $\widehat P_n$ and $\widehat P_m$ are independent
empirical measures based on $n$ and $m$ i.i.d.\ samples from $P$,
respectively, then
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\operatorname{W}^p_p(\widehat{P}_n,\widehat{P}_m)\gtrsim_pB\sqrt{\log(n+1)n^{-1}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    }

    }

<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
We construct $P$ as a discrete probability measure supported on $0=x_0<x_1<\cdots<x_L$. 

We denote $A_k=[x_k,\infty)$ and $S_k=P(A_k) $ for $k=1,\ldots,L$. The key observation, proved in
Appendix~\ref{appendix:proofoflemmaonedimensionalcaser=2p}, is that for
any two probability measures $\widehat P_n$ and $\widehat P_m$
supported on this grid,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname W_p^p(\widehat P_n,\widehat P_m)\ge\sum_{k=1}^{L}(x_k-x_{k-1})^p\left|\widehat P_n(A_k)-\widehat P_m(A_k)\right|.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Taking expectation, and noting that
$n\widehat{P}_n(A_k)\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(n,S_k)$
and
$m\widehat P_m(A_k)\sim\operatorname{Binomial}(m,S_k)
$ are independent, Lemma~\ref{lem:extendedlemma3.8ledoux} yields, provided
$0<S_k\le1/2$ and $nS_k\ge1,$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \label{eq:usefuleqconstructionr=2p}
\mathbb E\operatorname W_p^p(\widehat P_n,\widehat P_m)\gtrsim\sum_{k=1}^{L}(x_k-x_{k-1})^p \sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

This suggests constructing the support points $(x_k)_{k=1}^L$ and
the tail probabilities $(S_k)_{k=1}^L$ so that the following
conditions hold simultaneously:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\item The sequence $(S_k)_{k=1}^L$ is nonincreasing and defines valid tail
probabilities for a probability measure supported on $0=x_0<x_1<\cdots<x_L.$
\item For every $k=1,\ldots,L$,$0<S_k\le1/2$ and $nS_k\ge1,$ so that Lemma~\ref{lem:extendedlemma3.8ledoux} can be applied.
\item The resulting probability measure satisfies the moment  constraint.
\item The right-hand side of Eq.~\eqref{eq:usefuleqconstructionr=2p}
attains the desired lower-bound order $B\sqrt{\log(n+1)n^{-1}}.$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

We now make this choice more explicit. Set $S_{L+1}=0$. Then,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{\mathbb R}|x|^{2p}dP(x) =\sum_{k=1}^{L}(S_k-S_{k+1})x_k^{2p}\le\sum_{k=1}^{L}S_kx_k^{2p}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
A simple way to satisfy the moment constraint is therefore to
distribute the available budget equally among the $L$ terms by
choosing
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
S_kx_k^{2p}=\frac{B^2}{L},\qquad k=1,\ldots,L.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
If the support points are chosen in geometric progression,
$x_k=R2^k,$
then $x_k-x_{k-1}\ge x_k/2,$
and consequently
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
(x_k-x_{k-1})^p\sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}}\ge\frac{B}{2^p\sqrt{Ln}}.

Hence, summing over the $L$ scales gives
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

\sum_{k=1}^{L}(x_k-x_{k-1})^p
\sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}}\ge\frac{B\sqrt L}{2^p\sqrt n}.

It remains to choose $L$ and $R$ so that all the preceding
conditions are satisfied while allowing $L$ to be proportional to $\log(n+1)$. An explicit choice, together with the complete verification,
is given in Appendix~\ref{appendix:proofoflemmaonedimensionalcaser=2p}.

Set
$B\coloneqq b/\kappa_{p,d}.$
By Lemma~\ref{lem:onedimensionalcaser=2p}, there exists a probability
measure $P$ on $\mathbb R$ such that
$(\mathbb E_{X\sim P}|X|^{2p})^{1/2}\le B
$
and 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\operatorname W_p^p(\widehat P_n,\widehat P_m)\gtrsim_p B\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where $\widehat P_n$ and $\widehat P_m$ are independent empirical
measures based on $n$ and $m$ i.i.d.\ samples from $P$, respectively.

Let $T:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R^d$ be defined by $T(x)=xe_1$, and set
$\mu=\nu\coloneqq T_{\#}P.
$
For $\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$, writing
$
\theta_1=\langle e_1,\theta\rangle,
$
we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}
M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)^{1/2}\sigma(d\theta)=
\left(\mathbb E_{X\sim P}|X|^{2p}
\right)^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}
|\theta_1|^p\,\sigma(d\theta)\le
\kappa_{p,d}B=b.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*}

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Hence $\mu=\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)$.

Moreover, if
$
\widehat\mu_n=T_{\#}\widehat P_n
$
and
$\widehat\nu_m=T_{\#}\widehat P_m,$
then
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    }

\mathbb E
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\widehat\mu_n,\widehat\nu_m)=\kappa_{p,d}\mathbb E
\operatorname W_p^p(\widehat P_n,\widehat P_m)\gtrsim_p  b\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $\mu=\nu$,
$
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)=0.
$
Therefore,

\mathcal R_{n,m}^{\mathrm{plug}}
\left(\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)
\right)\gtrsim_p b\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.

<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:twopointmixtureversion}
Let $\mathcal A$ be a finite index set with $|\mathcal A|\ge 2$, and let
$$ \mu_0,\nu_0,\{\mu_\alpha:\alpha\in\mathcal A\}
\subseteq \mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)
$$
satisfy $\mu_0=\nu_0$ and
$$ \operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_\alpha,\nu_0) = \operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_\beta,\nu_0) >0
\quad \text{for all }\alpha,\beta\in\mathcal A.
$$
Fix any $\alpha_\star\in\mathcal A$ and define
$$ \mathsf P_0 \coloneqq \mu_0^{\otimes n},
\quad \mathsf P_1 \coloneqq \frac{1}{|\mathcal A|} \sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A}
\mu_\alpha^{\otimes n}.
$$
Then
$$ \mathcal R_{n,m}^{\star} \left(\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)\right) \ge \frac{\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_{\alpha_\star},\nu_0)}{4} \left[
1-\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf P_0,\mathsf P_1) \right].
$$
<!-- end lemma-10 -->

<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We first prove that
$ \mathcal K_{p,r}(b)\subseteq\mathcal P_p(\mathbb R^d).$
Define
$$\kappa_{p,d}\coloneqq \int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}} \left|\langle e_1,\theta\rangle\right|^pd\sigma(\theta),
$$
where $e_1=(1,0,\ldots,0)^\top\in\mathbb R^d$. By rotational invariance of $\sigma$ (see, for example,
\citet[Proposition~11.24]{peyre2025optimal}), for every
$x\in\mathbb R^d$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\begin{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\label{eq:spherical-moment-identity}
\int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}\left|\langle x,\theta\rangle\right|^p
d\sigma(\theta)=\kappa_{p,d}\|x\|^p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation} 
        {\end{equation}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Moreover, $\kappa_{p,d}>0$.

Let $\mu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and let $X\sim\mu$. Since the integrand
is nonnegative, Tonelli's theorem and
\eqref{eq:spherical-moment-identity} yield
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\kappa_{p,d}\,
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^p= \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu} \left[
\int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}} \left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^p d\sigma(\theta)
\right]= \int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu} \left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^p d\sigma(\theta).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $r\ge p$, the monotonicity of moments gives, for every
$\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$,
$$
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}
\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^p
\le \left( \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}
\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^r
\right)^{p/r} = M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}.
$$
Consequently,
$$ \kappa_{p,d} \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^p \le \int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r} d\sigma(\theta)\le b.
$$
Since $\kappa_{p,d}>0$, it follows that
$$
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^p \le \frac{b}{\kappa_{p,d}} <+\infty.
$$
Thus,
$
\mu\in\mathcal P_p(\mathbb R^d).
$

We next prove the inclusion for distributions with bounded Euclidean
$r$-th moments. Let $\mu\in\mathcal P_r(\mathbb R^d)$ satisfy $\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^r\le B.$
For every $\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$, the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality
gives
$$
|\langle X,\theta\rangle| \le \|X\|\|\theta\| = \|X\|.
$$
Therefore,
$$ M_{\mu,r}(\theta) =
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu} \left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^r \le \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^r \le B.
$$
Since $\sigma$ is a probability measure,
$$\int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r} d\sigma(\theta) \le B^{p/r}.
$$
Hence,
$$
\mu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}\left(B^{p/r}\right).
$$

Suppose now that $\operatorname{supp}(\mu)\subseteq\mathbb B(0,R).$
Then $\|X\|\le R$ almost surely, and hence
$$
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\|X\|^r\le R^r.
$$
Applying the preceding inclusion with $B=R^r$, we obtain
$$
\mu \in \mathcal K_{p,r} \left( (R^r)^{p/r} \right) = \mathcal K_{p,r}(R^p).
$$

Finally, let $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$. For every
$\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$, the triangle inequality for
$\operatorname{W}_p$ gives
$$ \operatorname{W}_p \left(
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu, \Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu
\right)\le \operatorname{W}_p
\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\delta_0
\right) + \operatorname{W}_p
\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu,\delta_0 \right).
$$
Using $(a+c)^p\le2^{p-1}(a^p+c^p) $ for $a,c\ge0, p\ge 1$, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{W}_p^p \left(
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu\right)&\le 2^{p-1} \operatorname{W}_p^p
\left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\delta_0
\right)+2^{p-1}
\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu,\delta_0\right)\\&=2^{p-1}
\mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|^p+2^{p-1}
\mathbb E_{Y\sim\nu}\left|\langle Y,\theta\rangle\right|^p\\&\le
2^{p-1} \left(
M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}+M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}\right),
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the last inequality follows again from $r\ge p$. Integrating
over $\mathbb S^{d-1}$ yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)
&\le 2^{p-1} \int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}
M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r} d\sigma(\theta)+
2^{p-1} \int_{\mathbb S^{d-1}}
M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r} d\sigma(\theta)\\
&\le 2^{p-1}b+2^{p-1}b=2^p b.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Fix $\theta\in \mathbb{S}^{d-1}$ such that $M_{Q,s}(\theta)<\infty$. Assume first that $s\neq 2q$. By applying \citet[Theorem~1]{fournier2015rate} in dimension one with moment order $s$, we obtain $$\mathbb E\left[\operatorname{W}_q^q \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right) \right]\le C_{q,s}\left(M_{Q,s}(\theta)\right)^{q/s} \left(n^{-1/2}+n^{-(s-q)/s}\right). $$ If $q<s<2q$, then $(s-q)/s<1/2$. Hence $n^{-1/2}\le n^{-(s-q)/s}.$ Therefore, $$ n^{-1/2}+n^{-(s-q)/s}\le2n^{-(s-q)/s}. $$ If $s>2q$, then $(s-q)/s>1/2$. Hence $n^{-(s-q)/s}\le n^{-1/2}.$ Therefore, $$ n^{-1/2}+n^{-(s-q)/s}\le 2n^{-1/2}. $$ Absorbing the factor $2$ into the constant $C_{q,s}$ gives the desired bound in both the first and the last regimes.

It remains to consider the critical case $s=2q$. This case is not stated explicitly in \citet[Theorem~1]{fournier2015rate}, but it follows from the argument in Step~2 in the proof of their Theorem~1. Since the ambient dimension is one and $q\ge1$, the condition $q>1/2$ required in that argument is automatically satisfied. We therefore obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left[\operatorname{W}_q^q \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right) \right] \lesssim_{q}M_{Q,s}\left(\theta\right)^{q/s} \sum_{\ell \ge 0}2^{q\ell}\min \left\{2^{-s\ell},\left(\frac{2^{-s\ell}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\right\}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $s=2q$, we have $2^{q\ell}2^{-s\ell}=2^{-q\ell}$, whereas
$2^{q\ell}\sqrt{2^{-s\ell}n^{-1}}=n^{-1/2}$. Thus
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left[\operatorname{W}_q^q \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right) \right]\lesssim_{q}M_{Q,s}(\theta)^{q/s}S_n,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where $S_n\coloneqq \sum_{\ell\ge 0} \min \left\{2^{-q\ell},n^{-1/2}\right\}$. For $n=1$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
S_1=\sum_{\ell \ge 0}2^{-q\ell}=\frac{1}{1-2^{-q}},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
which is bounded by a constant multiple of $\log 2$. Assume now that $n \ge 2$ and define
$$
L_n\coloneqq \left\lceil \frac{\log_2 n}{2q} \right\rceil.
$$
Since $n\ge2$, we have $\log_2 n>0$, and hence $L_n\ge1$. Moreover, by the definition of the ceiling function,
$$L_n\ge\frac{\log_2 n}{2q}.$$
Therefore,
$$ 2^{-qL_n} \le 2^{-q\frac{\log_2 n}{2q}}
= 2^{-\frac12\log_2 n} =n^{-1/2}.
$$
We split
$$ S_n = \sum_{\ell=0}^{L_n-1} \min\left\{
2^{-q\ell}, n^{-1/2} \right\} + \sum_{\ell=L_n}^{\infty} \min\left\{ 2^{-q\ell}, n^{-1/2} \right\}.
$$
We have
$$ \sum_{\ell=0}^{L_n-1}\min\left\{ 2^{-q\ell},
n^{-1/2} \right\} \le L_n n^{-1/2} \quad 
\sum_{\ell=L_n}^{\infty}
\min\left\{ 2^{-q\ell}, n^{-1/2} \right\}
\le \sum_{\ell=L_n}^{\infty}2^{-q\ell} =
\frac{2^{-qL_n}}{1-2^{-q}} \le \frac{1}{1-2^{-q}}n^{-1/2}.
$$
Combining the two estimates, we obtain
$$
S_n \le L_nn^{-1/2} + \frac{1}{1-2^{-q}}n^{-1/2} = \left( L_n+\frac{1}{1-2^{-q}}
\right)n^{-1/2}.
$$
Moreover, we also have
$$ L_n \le \frac{\log_2 n}{2q}+1 = \frac{\log n}{2q\log 2}+1 \le \frac{\log (n+1)}{2q\log 2}+1.
$$
As a consequence,
$$
S_n\le\left(\frac{\log(n+1)}{2q\log 2}+1+\frac{1}{1-2^{-q}}
\right)n^{-1/2}.
$$
Since $\log(n+1)\ge \log 2$ for every $n\ge1$, the constant terms can be absorbed into a multiple of $\log(n+1)$. Hence,
$$S_n\lesssim_{q}n^{-1/2}\log(n+1).
$$
Combining the preceding estimates yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left[\operatorname{W}^q_q\left(\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}Q\right)\right]\lesssim_{q}M_{Q,s}\left(\theta\right)^{q/s}n^{-1/2}\log(n+1)
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
when $s=2q$. This proves the desired estimate in all three regimes.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:sec5basicinequality}
Let $p> 1$. Then, for all $a,b\ge 0$,
$$|a^p-b^p|\le p2^{p-1}
\left(b^{p-1}|a-b|+|a-b|^p\right).
$$
Consequently, for all $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$,
$$
\left||x+y|^p-|x|^p\right|\le
p2^{p-1}\left(
|x|^{p-1}|y|+|y|^p\right).
$$
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We first prove the inequality for $a,b\ge0$. Consider the function
$f(t)=t^p$ on $[0,\infty)$. By the mean value theorem, there exists a point
$\xi$ between $a$ and $b$ such that
$$
a^p-b^p=f'( \xi )(a-b)=p\xi^{p-1}(a-b).
$$
Hence
$$|a^p-b^p|\le p|a-b|\max(a,b)^{p-1}.
$$
Moreover,
$$\max(a,b)\le b+|a-b|.
$$
Therefore,
$$|a^p-b^p| \le p|a-b| \left(b+|a-b|\right)^{p-1}.
$$
Using the elementary inequality
$$
(u+v)^{p-1} \le2^{p-1}\left(u^{p-1}+v^{p-1}\right),\qquad u,v\ge0,
$$
we obtain
$$ |a^p-b^p| \le p2^{p-1}|a-b| \left( b^{p-1}+|a-b|^{p-1} \right).
$$
Hence
$$
|a^p-b^p| \le p2^{p-1} \left( b^{p-1}|a-b|+|a-b|^p \right).
$$

Now take
$$ a=|x+y|, \qquad b=|x|.
$$
By the reverse triangle inequality,
$$|a-b|=\left||x+y|-|x|\right|\le|y|.
$$
Substituting this into the previous estimate gives
$$ \left||x+y|^p-|x|^p\right|\le p2^{p-1}\left(|x|^{p-1}|y|+|y|^p\right).
$$
This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Since $\theta \in \mathcal{G}$ and $r \ge p$, the projected population
measures have finite $p$-th moments. The projected empirical measures are finitely supported and hence also have finite $p$-th moments. Therefore, by the one-dimensional quantile representation of the Wasserstein distance (see, for example, \citet[Theorem~2.10]{bobkov2019one}), we have
$$
\operatorname{W}_p^p
\left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n, \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\nu}_m \right)
=\int_0^1\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)\right|^pdu
$$
and
$$
\operatorname{W}_p^p \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu, \Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu
\right)=\int_0^1\left|F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^pdu.
$$
Therefore,
$$
\left|\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\nu}_m \right)-\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu\right)\right|\le
\int_0^1\left|\left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)
\right|^p-\left|F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^p
\right|du.
$$

For each $u\in(0,1)$, set
$$
x(u)=F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)
$$
and
$$
y(u)=\left(F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right)
-\left(G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right).
$$
Then
$$
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)=
x(u)+y(u).
$$
Using Lemma~\ref{lem:sec5basicinequality}, we obtain
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\left|
\left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)
\right|^p-\left|F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^p\right|
\\
&\le
C_p\left|F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1}\left|\left(F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right)
-\left(G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right)\right|
\\
&\quad
+C_p\left|\left(F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right)-\left(G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right)\right|^p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$

Next, we use
$$
\left|F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1}\le C_p\left(|F_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}+|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\right).$$
Thus,
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\left|\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)\right|^p-\left|
F_\theta^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^p\right|
\\
&\le C_p \left(
|F_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}+|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\right)\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|
\\
&\quad
+C_p\left(|F_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}+
|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\right)
\left|G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|
\\
&\quad
+C_p\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^p
+C_p\left|G_{m,\theta}^{-1}(u)-G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
Integrating over $u\in(0,1)$ and taking expectation gives
$$
\mathbb{E}_{\mu,\nu}
\left|
\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\nu}_m
\right)-\operatorname{W}_p^p\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu,
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu\right)\right|
\lesssim_{p}\left(\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}+\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}
\right).
$$
This concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-12 -->

and

Similarly, a direct calculation gives, for every $x\in\mathbb{R}$,
