<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:cdf-level-interval}
Let $\mu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$, where $\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ is defined in Eq.~\eqref{sec1:eqclass}. Fix $\theta\in \mathcal{G}$, where $\mathcal{G}$ is defined in Eq.~\eqref{eq:gooddirections}, and let $F_\theta$ denote the distribution function of $\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu$. Then, for any $0<a<c<1$,
$$ \left| \left\{ x\in\mathbb R:
a<F_\theta(x)\le c\right\}
\right| \le 2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}
\max\left\{ a^{-1/r}, (1-c)^{-1/r} \right\},
$$
where $M_{\mu,r}(\theta) \coloneqq \mathbb E_{X\sim\mu}|\langle X,\theta\rangle|^r.
$
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
By the definition of $\mathcal{G}$, we have $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)<+\infty$. 

We first consider the degenerate case $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)=0$. In this case,
$$
|\langle X,\theta\rangle|=0
\quad\text{almost surely}.
$$
Hence
$$
F_\theta(x) =
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
0, & x<0,\\1, & x\ge0.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
Since $0<a<c<1$, we have
$$\left\{x\in\mathbb R:a<F_\theta(x)\le c\right\} = \emptyset.
$$
Therefore,
$$
\left| \left\{ x\in\mathbb R: a<F_\theta(x)\le c \right\} \right| =0.
$$
The desired inequality follows immediately in this case.

We now assume that $0<M_{\mu,r}(\theta)<+\infty$. Define
$$
R_a\coloneqq\left(\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{a}\right)^{1/r},\qquad
R_c\coloneqq
\left(\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{1-c}\right)^{1/r}.
$$
We claim that
$$\left\{x\in\mathbb R:
a<F_\theta(x)\le c
\right\}\subseteq(-R_a,R_c].
$$

Indeed, for every $x\le -R_a<0$, Markov's inequality gives
$$
F_\theta(x) =
\mathbb P_{X\sim\mu}\left(\langle X,\theta\rangle\le x\right) \le
\mathbb P_{X\sim\mu}\left(|\langle X,\theta\rangle|\ge |x|\right) \le
\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{|x|^r}
\le \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{R_a^r}
= a.
$$
Thus, if $F_\theta(x)>a$, then necessarily $x>-R_a$.

Similarly, for every $x>R_c>0$, Markov's inequality gives
$$
1-F_\theta(x) = \mathbb P_{X\sim\mu}\left(\langle X,\theta\rangle>x\right) \le \mathbb P_{X\sim\mu}\left(|\langle X,\theta\rangle|\ge x\right)
\le
\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{x^r}
< \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)}{R_c^r} = 1-c.
$$
Hence $F_\theta(x)>c$ for every $x>R_c$. Therefore, if $F_\theta(x)\le c$, then necessarily $x\le R_c$.

Combining the two observations, we obtain
$$
\left\{x\in\mathbb R:
a<F_\theta(x)\le c\right\}
\subset(-R_a,R_c].
$$
Consequently,
$$\left|\left\{
x\in\mathbb R:a<F_\theta(x)\le c
\right\}\right|
\le|(-R_a,R_c]|=R_a+R_c.
$$
Finally,
$$R_a+R_c
=M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}
\left(a^{-1/r}+(1-c)^{-1/r}
\right)\le
2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}\max\left\{
a^{-1/r},(1-c)^{-1/r}
\right\}.
$$
This proves the lemma.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The case $\alpha=0$ is trivial. Hence, throughout the proof, we assume that $0<\alpha\le 1$.

We first consider the degenerate case $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)=0$. In this case,
$$
|\langle X,\theta\rangle|=0
\quad\text{almost surely}.
$$
Hence, almost surely
$$
F_\theta(x)=F_{n,\theta}(x) =
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
0, & x<0,\\
1, & x\ge 0.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
Consequently, for every $u\in(0,1)$,
$$
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)=F_\theta^{-1}(u)=0.
$$
Since the right-hand side of the desired estimate is also equal to zero when $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)=0$, the result follows in this case.

We now assume that $M_{\mu,r}(\theta)\in(0,+\infty)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:Quantile-CDF}, we have
$$ \int_0^\alpha \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du=\int_{\mathbb R}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}
\right|dx.
$$
We split the proof into two regimes, according to whether $\alpha<1/4$ or $\alpha\ge1/4$.

Assume first that $0<\alpha<1/4$. We decompose $\mathbb R$ into the following three regions.

\textbf{Case 1.} Let
$$
J_1=\left\{x\in\mathbb R:F_\theta(x)\le 2\alpha\right\}.
$$
For every $x\in J_1$, we have
$$
\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}
\right|\le\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_\theta(x)\right|.
$$
Therefore,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}
-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|
&\le\mathbb E\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_\theta(x)\right| \\
&\le\sqrt{\frac{F_\theta(x)(1-F_\theta(x))}{n}} \\
&\le\sqrt{\frac{F_\theta(x)}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

For $j\ge0$, define
$$
D_j
\coloneqq\left\{x\in\mathbb R:2^{-j}\alpha<F_\theta(x)\le 2^{-j+1}\alpha\right\}.
$$
The sets $(D_j)_{j\ge0}$ cover the region $\{x\in\mathbb R:0<F_\theta(x)\le 2\alpha\}$, while the set where $F_\theta(x)=0$ does not contribute to the integral. Hence,
\allowdisplaybreaks
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{J_1}\mathbb E\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}
-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|dx 
&\le\int_{J_1}\sqrt{\frac{F_\theta(x)}{n}}dx\\
&\le\sum_{j\ge0}\int_{D_j}\sqrt{\frac{F_\theta(x)}{n}}
dx\\
&\le\sum_{j\ge0}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-j+1}\alpha}{n}}|D_j| \\
&\le\sum_{j\ge0}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-j+1}\alpha}{n}}2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}\left(\min\{2^{-j}\alpha,1-2^{-j+1}\alpha\}
\right)^{-1/r} \\
&\le\sum_{j\ge0}\sqrt{\frac{2^{-j+1}\alpha}{n}}2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}(2^{-j}\alpha)^{-1/r} \\
&\lesssim_{r}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n}
\alpha^{1/2-1/r}\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-j(1/2-1/r)} \\
&\lesssim_{r}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n}\alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Here we used Lemma~\ref{lem:cdf-level-interval}. Moreover, since $\alpha<1/4$, we have
$$
2^{-j}\alpha<\frac14
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
1-2^{-j+1}\alpha>\frac12,
$$
so that
$$
\min\{2^{-j}\alpha,1-2^{-j+1}\alpha\}=2^{-j}\alpha.
$$
Besides, the last inequality follows from the summability of
$\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-j(1/2-1/r)}$, since $r>2$.

\textbf{Case 2.} Let
$$
J_2=\left\{x\in\mathbb R:2\alpha<F_\theta(x)\le\frac34\right\}.
$$
For $x\in J_2$, we have $F_\theta(x)>\alpha$, and hence
$$
\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}=\alpha.
$$
Therefore,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-
\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|
&=\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-\alpha\right| \\
&=\left(\alpha-F_{n,\theta}(x)\right)_+ \\
&\le\alpha\mathbf 1_{\{F_{n,\theta}(x)\le \alpha\}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $F_\theta(x)>2\alpha$, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-
\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|
&\le\alpha\mathbb P\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha\right) \\
&\le\alpha\mathbb P\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\frac{F_\theta(x)}{2}\right) \\
&\le\alpha\exp\left(-\frac n8 F_\theta(x)\right) \\
&\le\alpha\exp\left(-\frac{n\alpha}{4}\right).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
The third inequality follows from a Chernoff bound for Bernoulli sums; see, for example, the proof of \citet[Corollary~2.3.4]{Vershynin_2026}.

Combining this estimate with Lemma~\ref{lem:cdf-level-interval}, we get
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{J_2}\mathbb{E} \left| \min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}- \min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}
\right|dx &
\le 2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} \alpha\exp\left(-\frac{n\alpha}{4}\right)\left(\min\left\{2\alpha,\frac14\right\}\right)^{-1/r} \\
&\le C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} \alpha^{1-1/r} \exp\left(-\frac{n\alpha}{4}\right) \\
&=C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}\frac{\alpha^{1/2-1/r}}{\sqrt n}\sqrt{n\alpha}\exp\left(-\frac{n\alpha}{4}\right) \\
&\lesssim_{r}\frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
In the second inequality, we used $\alpha<1/4$, which implies $\min\left\{2\alpha,\frac14\right\}\ge \alpha.$
The last inequality follows from the fact that $
\sup_{y\ge0}\sqrt y e^{-y/4}<+\infty.$

\textbf{Case 3.} Let
$$
J_3=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\frac34<F_\theta(x)\le1\right\}.
$$
As in Case 2, for $x\in J_3$, we have
$$
\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}=\alpha
$$
and therefore
$$
\left| \min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|\le\alpha\mathbf 1_{\{F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha\}}.
$$
Since $F_\theta(x)>3/4$ and $\alpha<1/4$, we have
$$F_\theta(x)-\alpha>\frac12.
$$
By Chebyshev's inequality,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb P(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le\alpha)
&\le \mathbb P\left( |F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_\theta(x)|
\ge F_\theta(x)-\alpha
\right) \\
&\le\mathbb P\left(|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_\theta(x)|\ge
\frac{1}{2}\right) \\
&\le 4\operatorname{Var}(F_{n,\theta}(x)) \\
&=\frac{4F_\theta(x)(1-F_\theta(x))}{n} \\
&\le\frac{4(1-F_\theta(x))}{n}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Thus,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}}

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{J_3}\mathbb E\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\}\right|dx 
&\le\frac{4\alpha}{n}\int_{J_3}\left(1-F_\theta(x)\right)dx.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

For $j\ge0$, define
$$
E_j \coloneqq \left\{ x\in\mathbb R: 1-2^{-j-2}<F_\theta(x)\le 1-2^{-j-3} \right\}.
$$
Then
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{J_3}\left(1-F_\theta(x)\right)dx
\le \sum_{j\ge0}\int_{E_j}\left(1-F_\theta(x)\right)dx
\le\sum_{j\ge0}2^{-j-2}|E_j|.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
By Lemma~\ref{lem:cdf-level-interval},
$$ |E_j| \le 2M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} \left(\min\{1-2^{-j-2},2^{-j-3}\}\right)^{-1/r}.$$
Since $\min\{1-2^{-j-2},2^{-j-3}\}=2^{-j-3}$,
we get
$$
|E_j|\le C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}2^{j/r}.
$$
Therefore,
$$
\int_{J_3}\left(1-F_\theta(x)\right)dx
\le C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} \sum_{j\ge0}2^{-j}2^{j/r}\le C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r},
$$
where the last inequality follows from $1-1/r>0$. Consequently,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}}

        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{J_3}\mathbb E \left| \min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),\alpha\}
-\min\{F_\theta(x),\alpha\} \right|dx
&\lesssim_{r}\frac{\alpha}{n}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} \\
&\lesssim_{r} \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
In the last inequality, we used $n^{-1}\le n^{-1/2}$ and, since $r>2$ and $0<\alpha<1$, we have
$$
\alpha\le \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$

It remains to consider the case $\alpha\ge1/4$. In this case, we use the direct bound
$$ \mathbb E\int_0^\alpha \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du \le \mathbb E\int_0^1
\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|du=\mathbb E \left[
\operatorname W_1 \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat\mu_n,
\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu
\right)
\right].
$$
Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} applied with $q=1$, 
$$
\mathbb E\int_0^\alpha \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|du\le C_rM_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}n^{-1/2}.
$$
Since $\alpha\ge 1/4$ and $1/2-1/r>0$, we have
$$ \alpha^{1/2-1/r} \ge \left(\frac14\right)^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
Equivalently,
$$
1 \le 4^{1/2-1/r}\alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
Thus,
$$
M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}n^{-1/2} \le 4^{1/2-1/r} \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
Absorbing the factor $4^{1/2-1/r}$ into the constant $C_r$, we obtain
$$ \mathbb E\int_0^\alpha \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|du
\lesssim_r \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}}{\sqrt n} \alpha^{1/2-1/r}.
$$
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The case $p=1$ has already been established in the discussion of Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}. We therefore assume that $p>1$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:decomposition}, for each fixed $\theta\in \mathcal{G}$, the one-dimensional error is bounded by
$C_p\left(\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}+\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}+\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}\right).
$
We first control the terms $\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}$ and $\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}, since $r>2p$, we have
$$\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}\lesssim_{p,r} \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}}{\sqrt n},
\qquad
\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}\lesssim_{p,r} \frac{M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}}{\sqrt m}.
$$
Therefore, using $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and $n\le m$,
$$
\int_{\mathcal{G}} \mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}d\sigma(\theta) \lesssim_{p,r} \frac{b}{\sqrt n},
\qquad
\int_\mathcal{G}\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}d\sigma(\theta)\lesssim_{p,r} \frac{b}{\sqrt n}.
$$

It remains to control the weighted terms $\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}$ and $\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:weighted-quantile}, we have
$$\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}\lesssim_{p,r} \frac{M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}
+M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}
}{\sqrt n}.$$
Similarly,
$$\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}\lesssim_{p,r}\frac{M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}+M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}}{\sqrt m}.
$$

We now integrate these estimates over $\mathcal{G}$. First,
$$\int_\mathcal{G} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}d\sigma(\theta)\le b,
\quad\int_\mathcal{G} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}d\sigma(\theta)\le b.
$$
Moreover, by Hölder's inequality,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_\mathcal{G}
M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}
d\sigma(\theta)&\le\left(\int_\mathcal{G} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}\,d\sigma(\theta)\right)^{1/p}\left(
\int_\mathcal{G} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}\,d\sigma(\theta)
\right)^{(p-1)/p}\\
&\le b.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

Similarly, we have
$$
\int_{\mathcal{G}} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}d\sigma(\theta)\le b.
$$
Consequently, using again $n\le m$,
$$
\int_\mathcal{G}\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}\,d\sigma(\theta)\lesssim_{p,r}\frac{b}{\sqrt n},
\qquad
\int_\mathcal{G}\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}\,d\sigma(\theta)\lesssim_{p,r}\frac{b}{\sqrt n}.
$$

Combining the estimates for $\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}$, $\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}$, $\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}$, and $\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}$, we obtain
$$
\mathbb E\left|
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\widehat\mu_n,\widehat\nu_m)
-\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)
\right|\lesssim_{p,r} b n^{-1/2}.
$$
We complete the proof by taking the supremum over $\mu,\nu \in \mathcal{K}_{p,r}(b)$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
We prove the first estimate. The second estimate follows by taking the weight distribution to be $\mu$ instead of $\nu$, and the estimates involving $G_{m,\theta}^{-1}$ are obtained by exchanging the roles of $(\mu,n,F^{-1}_\theta,F^{-1}_{n,\theta})$ and $(\nu,m,G^{-1}_\theta,G^{-1}_{m,\theta})$.

If $M_{\nu,r}(\theta)=0$, then $G_\theta^{-1}(u)=0$ for every $u\in(0,1)$. Consequently, the desired estimate follows immediately. Hence, we assume that $M_{\nu,r}(\theta)\in(0,+\infty).$
Set $K\coloneqq M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}>0.$ Let $J_n\ge1$ be an integer to be chosen later. We decompose $(0,1)$ according to the size of $|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|$. Define
$$E^*\coloneqq\left\{u\in(0,1): |G_\theta^{-1}(u)|<K\right\},$$ for $0\le k\le J_n-1$,
$$E_k \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): 2^kK\le |G_\theta^{-1}(u)|<2^{k+1}K \right\},$$
and $$E_\infty\coloneqq \left\{u\in(0,1):|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|\ge 2^{J_n}K
\right\}.$$
Then $$(0,1)\subset E^*\cup\bigcup_{k=0}^{J_n-1}E_k\cup E_\infty.
$$

\textbf{Step 1: Control on the region $E^*$.} 
On $E^*$, we have $$|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1} \le K^{p-1}= M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}.$$
Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} (with $q=1$ and $s=r$), we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\int_{E^*}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1}du &\le
M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}
\mathbb E
\int_0^1\left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|
du\\
& \le C_r M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}\rho_n,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where
$$
\rho_n=
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\begin{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
n^{-(r-1)/r}, & 1<r<2,\\
n^{-1/2}\log(n+1), &r=2,\\
n^{-1/2}, & r>2.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@cases} 
        {\end{cases}} 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@cases\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
It is worth noting that $\rho_n$ is always bounded by the desired rate in the regime $p<r<2p$. Indeed:
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\begin{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\item If $1<r<2$, then, since $p>1$, we have
$
n^{-(r-1)/r}\le n^{-(r-p)/r}.
$
\item If $r>2$, then, since $r<2p$, we have
$
n^{-1/2}\le n^{-(r-p)/r}.
$
\item If $r=2$, then the condition $p<r<2p$ implies $1<p<2$. In this case, for all $n\ge1$,
$$
\log(n+1)\le \frac{2}{p-1}n^{(p-1)/2}.
$$
Therefore,
$$
n^{-1/2}\log(n+1)
\le
\frac{2}{p-1}n^{-(2-p)/2}.
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@itemize} 
        {\end{itemize}} 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@itemize\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Consequently, in all cases under the condition $p<r<2p$, we have
$\rho_n\le C_{p,r}n^{-(r-p)/r}.
$
Hence
$$\mathbb E \int_{E^*} \left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|
\left| G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du
\le C_{p,r} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r} n^{-(r-p)/r}.$$
\textbf{Step 2: Control on the dyadic regions $E_k$, $0\le k\le J_n-1$.}
On $E_k$, we have
$|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\le(2^{k+1}K)^{p-1}.\\
$
Define $$A_k \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): |G_\theta^{-1}(u)|\ge 2^kK \right\}.$$

We split the estimate of the shell contribution into three cases.

First, assume that $r>2$. Using Proposition~\ref{prop:peelingtool} and following the same process in Lemma~\ref{lem:weighted-quantile}, we obtain
$$\mathbb E \int_{E_k} \left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u) \right|\left| G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1} du \lesssim_{p,r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}n^{-1/2} 2^{k(p-r/2)}.$$
As a result, summing over $0\le k\le J_n-1$, and using $p-r/2>0$, we get
$$\sum_{k=0}^{J_n-1} \mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1} du \lesssim_{p,r} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}
n^{-1/2}2^{J_n(p-r/2)}.$$

Second, assume that $1 <r < 2$. In this case, we use Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}. To be more specific, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E \int_{E_k} \left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right| \left| G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1} du &\le 2^{(k+1)(p-1)}K^{p-1}\mathbb{E}\int_{E_k}\left|
F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right| du\\
& \le 2^{(k+1)(p-1)}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}\mathbb{E}\left(\operatorname{W}_1(\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\mu)\right)\\
& \lesssim_{p,r}2^{k(p-1)} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}n^{-(r-1)/r}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Summing over $0\le k \le J_n-1$, and using $p>1$, we obtain
$$\sum_{k=0}^{J_n-1} \mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)
\right|^{p-1} du \lesssim_{p,r} M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r} M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}n^{-(r-1)/r}2^{J_n(p-1)}.$$

Third, assume that $r=2$. Since $p<r<2p$, we have $1<p<2$. Therefore, we can choose $q_0=q_0(p)$ such that $1<q_0<2/(3-p).$

On $E_k$, we have
$$|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|^{p-1}\le(2^{k+1}K)^{p-1}.$$
Denote
$$A_k=\left\{u\in(0,1):|G_\theta^{-1}(u)|\ge 2^kK\right\}.
$$
Then $E_k\subset A_k$. Moreover, for the Lebesgue measure $\lambda$ on $(0,1)$, we also have
$$\lambda(A_k)=\mathbb P_{U\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)}(U\in A_k)=
\mathbb P_{U\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)}\left(\left|G_\theta^{-1}(U)\right|\ge 2^kK\right)=\mathbb P_{Y\sim\nu}
\left(|\langle Y,\theta\rangle|\ge 2^kK
\right).
$$
In the last equality, we used the fact that if $U\sim\operatorname{Unif}(0,1)$, then $G_\theta^{-1}(U)$ has distribution $\Pi^\theta_{\#}\nu$. \\
Then, using Markov's inequality, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\lambda(A_k) \le \frac{M_{\nu,2}(\theta)}{(2^{k}K)^2}=2^{-2k}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

By Hölder's inequality with exponent $q_0$, we have
$$
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned}

        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du
&\le\int_{A_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du \\
&\le\lambda(A_k)^{1-1/q_0}\left(\int_0^1
\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{q_0}du
\right)^{1/q_0}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
Taking expectation and using Jensen's inequality, we obtain
$$ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    \mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du
&\le\lambda(A_k)^{1-1/q_0}\left[\mathbb E\int_0^1
\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{q_0}du
\right]^{1/q_0} \\
&=\lambda(A_k)^{1-1/q_0}\left[\mathbb E\operatorname{W}_{q_0}^{q_0}
\left(\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu
\right)\right]^{1/q_0}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
Since $q_0>1$ and $r=2$, we have $q_0<2<2q_0.$ Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} applied with $q=q_0$ and $s=2$,
$$\mathbb E\operatorname{W}_{q_0}^{q_0} \left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{\mu}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}\mu \right) 
\lesssim_{q_0}M_{\mu,2}(\theta)^{q_0/2}n^{-(2-q_0)/2}.
$$
Combining this estimate with $\lambda(A_k)\le 2^{-2k}$ gives
$$\mathbb E \int_{E_k} \left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du\lesssim_{q_0} M_{\mu,2}(\theta)^{1/2} 2^{-2k(1-1/q_0)}n^{-(2-q_0)/(2q_0)}.
$$
Consequently,
$$ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|
\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du \\
&\quad\lesssim_{p,q_0}M_{\mu,2}(\theta)^{1/2}M_{\nu,2}(\theta)^{(p-1)/2}n^{-(2-q_0)/(2q_0)}2^{k(p-3+2/q_0)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\end{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
$$
By our choice of $q_0$, we have $p-3+\frac{2}{q_0}>0.$ Hence,
$$ \sum_{k=0}^{J_n-1} 2^{k(p-3+2/q_0)}\lesssim_{p,q_0} 2^{J_n(p-3+2/q_0)}.
$$
Therefore,
$$ 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@aligned} 
        {\begin{aligned}} 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@aligned\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
&\sum_{k=0}^{J_n-1}\mathbb E\int_{E_k}\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du \\
&\quad\lesssim_{p,q_0}M_{\mu,2}(\theta)^{1/2}M_{\nu,2}(\theta)^{(p-1)/2}n^{-(2-q_0)/(2q_0)}2^{J_n(p-3+2/q_0)}.

With this choice, each term in the bracket is bounded by a constant multiple of $n^{-(r-p)/r}$. Therefore,
$$\mathbb E\int_0^1\left|F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|
\left|G_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|^{p-1}du\lesssim_{p,r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{1/r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{(p-1)/r}n^{-(r-p)/r}.
$$

<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The case $p=1$ follows directly from Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}
and the reverse triangle inequality for $\operatorname{W}_1$. We therefore assume that $p>1$.

The proof then follows the same aggregation argument as in the regime
$r>2p$. Indeed, Lemma~\ref{lem:decomposition} reduces the directional error
to the four terms $\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}$, $\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}$, $\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}$, and
$\mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate}, since
$p<r<2p$,
$$\mathsf{C}_{n,\theta}
\lesssim_{p,r}M_{\mu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}n^{-(r-p)/r},
\qquad \mathsf{D}_{m,\theta}
\lesssim_{p,r}M_{\nu,r}(\theta)^{p/r}m^{-(r-p)/r},
$$
while Lemma~\ref{lem:truncated-weighted-quantile} gives the same
sample-size rates for $\mathsf{A}_{n,\theta}$ and $\mathsf{B}_{m,\theta}$.

Integrating over $\mathcal{G}$ and applying Hölder's inequality exactly as in the
proof for the regime $r>2p$, together with
$\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ and $n\le m$, yields
$$
\mathbb E\left|
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\widehat\mu_n,\widehat\nu_m)-
\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)
\right|\lesssim_{p,r}b\,n^{-(r-p)/r}.
$$
Taking the supremum over $\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,r}(b)$ completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-18 -->

Fix $\theta\in\mathbb S^{d-1}$ such that $M_{Q,2q}(\theta)<\infty$. If $\mathbb{E}_{X \sim Q}\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle \right|^{2q}=0$, then $\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}Q=\delta_{0}$ and $\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n=\delta_{0}$ almost surely. Hence the desired estimate is immediate. Thus, in the sequel, we assume that $\mathbb{E}_{X \sim Q}\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle \right|^{2q}>0$.

Applying \citet[Proposition~7.14]{bobkov2019one} then using Tonelli's theorem, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left[\operatorname{W}_q^q\left( \Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n,\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q\right) \right] \le q2^{q-1}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty} \left|x\right|^{q-1}\mathbb{E}\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right|dx, 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

where $F_{\theta}$ and $F_{n,\theta}$ denote the cumulative distribution functions of $\Pi^\theta_{\#}Q$ and $\Pi^\theta_{\#}\widehat{Q}_n$, respectively. We emphasize that this notation is used only within the present proof.

Following the proof of \citet[Corollary~7.18]{bobkov2019one}, we obtain

Denote $T(u) \coloneqq \mathbb{P}_{X\sim Q}\left(\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle \right|\ge u\right)$. Then, for every $x\in\mathbb{R}$, we have

Moreover, the function $s \mapsto \min \left\{2s,\sqrt{\dfrac{s}{n}}\right\}$ is nondecreasing on $[0,\infty)$. Therefore,

&= q2^{q}\int_{0}^{+\infty}x^{q-1}\min \left\{2T\left(x\right),\sqrt{\frac{T\left(x\right)}{n}}\right\}dx.

M_{\theta}\coloneqq M_{\mu,2p}(\theta) +M_{\nu,2p}(\theta).

If $M_{\theta}=0$, then $M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)=M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)=0$. Thus, $F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)=G^{-1}_{\theta}(u)=0$ for every $u \in (0,1)$ and the desired estimate follows immediately. We therefore assume that $M_{\theta}>0.$

Define
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
H_{\theta}(u)\coloneqq \max \left\{\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|, \left|G^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right| \right\}, \quad u \in (0,1).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

To obtain the desired estimate, it is enough to show that
$$\mathbb{E}\displaystyle \int_{0}^{1}\left|F^{-1}_{n,\theta}(u)-F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|H_{\theta}(u)^{p-1}du\lesssim_{p}\left(M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)+M_{\nu,2p}(\theta)\right)^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.$$
We decompose $(0,1)$ according to the size of $H_{\theta}(u)$. Define
$$E^* \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): H_\theta(u)< M^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}
\right\},
$$
and, for $k\ge0$,
$$ E_k \coloneqq \left\{ u\in(0,1): 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\le H_\theta(u)<2^{k+1}M^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\right\}.$$
Then $$(0,1)=E^*\cup\bigcup_{k\ge0}E_k.$$

\textbf{Step 1: Control on the region $E^*$.} 
On $E^*$, we have

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
H^{p-1}_{\theta}(u)\le M_{\theta}^{(p-1)/(2p)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Therefore, by Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-rate} (with $q=1$, $s=2p$), we get
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}}

\mathbb{E}\int_{E^*}\left|F^{-1}_{n,\theta}(u)-F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|H^{p-1}_{\theta}(u) du \lesssim_{p}M_{\theta}^{(p-1)/(2p)}\left(M_{\mu,2p}(\theta)\right)^{1/(2p)}n^{-1/2}\lesssim_{p}M^{1/2}_{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{\log (n+1)}{n}}.

\textbf{Step 2: Control on the remaining regions $E_k$ for $k \ge 0$.}
In this step, we first need the following auxiliary lemma.

\medskip\noindent\textbf{Auxiliary lemma.} For $k\ge 0$, we set
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

L_{k}\coloneqq \left\{u \in (0,1):H_{\theta}(u)\ge 2^kM_{\theta}^{1/(2p)}\right\}.

Let $S_k\coloneqq \lambda(L_k)$ where $\lambda$ denotes Lebesgue measure on $(0,1)$. Define

Then there exists a universal constant $C>0$ such that, for every $k\ge 0$,

    [Proof of the auxiliary lemma]
Since $F^{-1}_{\theta}$ and $G^{-1}_{\theta}$ are non-decreasing, each set of the form
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left\{u \in (0,1):\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|\ge 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\right\} \quad \text{or} \quad \left\{u \in (0,1):\left|G^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|\ge 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\right\}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
is the union of a left endpoint interval and a right endpoint interval. Therefore, $L_k$ is the union of at most one left endpoint interval and at most one right endpoint interval. We treat only the left endpoint since the right endpoint interval can be handled similarly.

Let $a_k$ denote the length of the left endpoint part of $L_k$. Then, by the definition, we have $0 \le a_k\le S_{k}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:Quantile-CDF} and Tonelli's theorem, we have
$$ \mathbb{E}\int_0^{a_k} \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du=\int_{\mathbb R}\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|dx.
$$
We split the integral in $x$ into two regions: $\left|x\right| \le 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}$ and $\left|x\right| > 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}$.

Since the map $y \mapsto \min\{y,a_k\}$ is $1$-Lipschitz, for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right| \le \mathbb{E}\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right|\le \sqrt{\frac{F_{\theta}(x)(1-F_{\theta}(x))}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
If $F_{\theta}(x) \le 2S_k$, then 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}}

    }

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right| \le \sqrt{\frac{2S_k}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
If $F_{\theta}(x)>2S_k$, then $a_k\le S_k<F_{\theta}(x)/2$. As a consequence, 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    }

\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|=\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-a_k
\right| \le a_k\mathbbm{1}_{\left\{F_{n,\theta}(x)\le a_k\right\}}.

    }

Since $a_k \le S_k$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

        {

        {

\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|\le S_k\mathbb{P}\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le S_k\right).
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

Using $S_k \le F_{\theta}(x) /2$ and Chernoff's inequality (see, for example, the proof of \citet[Corollary~2.3.4]{Vershynin_2026}), we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*}

    }

\mathbb{P}\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le S_k\right) \le \mathbb{P}\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le F_{\theta}(x)/2\right)\le \exp\left(-cnF_\theta(x)\right)\le \exp(-cnS_k).

Thus,

S_k\mathbb{P}\left(F_{n,\theta}(x)\le S_k\right) \le S_k\exp\left(-cnS_k\right)=\sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}}\sqrt{nS_k} \exp\left(-cnS_k\right)\le C\sqrt{\frac{S_k}{n}},

where we used the fact that the function $z\mapsto \sqrt ze^{-cz}$ is bounded on $[0,+\infty)$.
On the other hand, we also have

\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|\le a_k \le S_k.

Consequently, combining the estimates above, for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have

\mathbb{E}
\left|\min\left\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\right\}-\min\left\{F_{\theta}(x),a_k\right\}\right|
\le Cm_k.

\int_{\left|x\right|\le 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}}\mathbb{E}\left|\min\left\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\right\}-\min\left\{F_{\theta}(x),a_k\right\}\right|dx \le C2^kM_{\theta}^{1/(2p)}m_k.

For the second region, as we did in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-ratev2}, following the proof in \citet[Corollary~7.17]{bobkov2019one}, we have

\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|& \le \mathbb{E}\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right|\\
& \le \min \left\{2F_{\theta}(x)\left(1-F_{\theta}(x)\right),\sqrt{\frac{F_{\theta}(x)\left(1-F_{\theta}(x)\right)}{n}}\right\}\\
& \le \min \left\{2T(\left|x\right|),\sqrt{\frac{T(\left|x\right|)}{n}}\right\},

where $T(t)=\mathbb{P}_{X\sim\mu}\left(\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|\ge t\right)$ for $t\ge0$.

Now, suppose that $2^jM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}< \left|x\right|\le 2^{j+1}M^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}$ for some $j \ge k$. Since $\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right| \le H_{\theta}(u)$ for all $u\in (0,1)$, we have

\min \left\{2T\left(\left|x\right|\right),\sqrt{\frac{T\left(\left|x\right|\right)}{n}}\right\} \le \min \left\{2S_j,\sqrt{\frac{S_j}{n}}\right\}\le  2\min \left\{S_j,\sqrt{\frac{S_j}{n}}\right\}=2m_j.

is equal to $2^{j+1}M_{\theta}^{1/(2p)}$. Hence,

&\int_{\left|x\right|>2^kM_\theta^{1/(2p)}}
\mathbb E\left|\min\left\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\right\}-\min\left\{F_\theta(x),a_k\right\}\right|dx \\
&\quad =\sum_{j\ge k}\int_{2^jM_\theta^{1/(2p)}<\left|x\right|\le 2^{j+1}M_\theta^{1/(2p)}}\mathbb E\left|\min\left\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\right\}-\min\left\{F_\theta(x),a_k\right\}
\right|dx \\
&\quad \lesssim M_\theta^{1/(2p)} \sum_{j\ge k}2^jm_j.

Let $J_n$ be a positive integer, to be chosen later. For $j \le J_n$, using the definition of $m_j$ and Cauchy--Schwarz inequality, we have

\sum_{j=0}^{J_n}2^{jp}m_j \le \sum_{j=0}^{J_n}\sqrt{\frac{2^{2pj}S_j}{n}} \le \sqrt{\frac{J_n+1}{n}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{J_n}2^{2pj}S_j\right)^{1/2}\lesssim_{p}\sqrt{\frac{J_n+1}{n}}.

On the other hand, for $j>J_n$, we have

Choose $J_n\coloneqq \left\lceil \frac{\log_2(n+1)}{2p}\right\rceil
$. By the definition of the ceiling function,

J_n+1 \le \frac{\log(n+1)}{2p\log 2} +2 \lesssim_{p}\log(n+1),

where we use $\log(n+1) \ge \log 2$. Moreover,

2^{-pJ_n} \le 2^{-1/2 \log_2(n+1)}=(n+1)^{-1/2}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.

\sqrt{\frac{J_n+1}{n}} +2^{-pJ_n}\lesssim_{p}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.

Hence,

\sum_{k\ge 0}\mathbb{E}\int_{E_k}\left|F^{-1}_{n,\theta}(u)-F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|H_{\theta}(u)^{p-1}du\lesssim_{p}M^{1/2}_{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}.

Since $F^{-1}_{\theta}$ and $G^{-1}_{\theta}$ are non-decreasing, each set of the form
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left\{u \in (0,1):\left|F^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|\ge 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\right\} \quad \text{or} \quad \left\{u \in (0,1):\left|G^{-1}_{\theta}(u)\right|\ge 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}\right\}
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
is the union of a left endpoint interval and a right endpoint interval. Therefore, $L_k$ is the union of at most one left endpoint interval and at most one right endpoint interval. We treat only the left endpoint since the right endpoint interval can be handled similarly.

Let $a_k$ denote the length of the left endpoint part of $L_k$. Then, by the definition, we have $0 \le a_k\le S_{k}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:Quantile-CDF} and Tonelli's theorem, we have
$$ \mathbb{E}\int_0^{a_k} \left| F_{n,\theta}^{-1}(u)-F_\theta^{-1}(u)\right|du=\int_{\mathbb R}\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right|dx.
$$
We split the integral in $x$ into two regions: $\left|x\right| \le 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}$ and $\left|x\right| > 2^kM^{1/(2p)}_{\theta}$.

Since the map $y \mapsto \min\{y,a_k\}$ is $1$-Lipschitz, for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right| \le \mathbb{E}\left|F_{n,\theta}(x)-F_{\theta}(x)\right|\le \sqrt{\frac{F_{\theta}(x)(1-F_{\theta}(x))}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
If $F_{\theta}(x) \le 2S_k$, then 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|
\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}
\right| \le \sqrt{\frac{2S_k}{n}}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
If $F_{\theta}(x)>2S_k$, then $a_k\le S_k<F_{\theta}(x)/2$. As a consequence, 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\left|\min\{F_{n,\theta}(x),a_k\}-\min\{F_\theta(x),a_k\}

For the second region, as we did in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:projected-empirical-ratev2}, following the proof in \citet[Corollary~7.17]{bobkov2019one}, we have

& \le \min \left\{2F_{\theta}(x)\left(1-F_{\theta}(x)\right),\sqrt{\frac{F_{\theta}(x)\left(1-F_{\theta}(x)\right)}{n}}\right\}\\
& \le \min \left\{2T(\left|x\right|),\sqrt{\frac{T(\left|x\right|)}{n}}\right\},

where $T(t)=\mathbb{P}_{X\sim\mu}\left(\left|\langle X,\theta\rangle\right|\ge t\right)$ for $t\ge0$.

\min \left\{2T\left(\left|x\right|\right),\sqrt{\frac{T\left(\left|x\right|\right)}{n}}\right\} \le \min \left\{2S_j,\sqrt{\frac{S_j}{n}}\right\}\le  2\min \left\{S_j,\sqrt{\frac{S_j}{n}}\right\}=2m_j.

Moreover, the Lebesgue measure of the shell

<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Conditioning on $\widetilde N$ and using Jensen's inequality with the convex function $z\mapsto |z|$, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb E\left[ \left| \frac{\widetilde N}{n} - \frac{\widetilde M}{m}
\right| \middle| \widetilde N
\right] &\ge\left|\frac{\widetilde N}{n} -
\mathbb E\left[\frac{\widetilde M}{m}\middle|\widetilde N \right] \right|.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $\widetilde N$ and $\widetilde M$ are independent, we have
$$ \mathbb E\left[\frac{\widetilde M}{m}\middle| \widetilde N
\right] = \mathbb E\left[\frac{\widetilde M}{m} \right]= s.
$$
Therefore,
$$
\mathbb E\left[ \left|\frac{\widetilde N}{n} - \frac{\widetilde M}{m} \right| \middle|\widetilde N\right] \ge \left| \frac{\widetilde N}{n}-s
\right|.
$$
Taking expectation with respect to $\widetilde N$, we get
$$\mathbb E\left| \frac{\widetilde N}{n}- \frac{\widetilde M}{m}
\right| \ge \mathbb E\left|\frac{\widetilde N}{n}-s \right|= \frac{1}{n}\mathbb E|\widetilde N-ns|.$$
By \citet[Lemma~3.8]{bobkov2019one}, there exists a universal constant $c_0>0$ such that
$$\mathbb E|\widetilde N-ns| \ge c_0 \min\{2ns(1-s),\sqrt{ns(1-s)}\}.
$$
Since $0<s\le1/2$, we have $1-s\ge1/2$. Moreover, since $ns\ge1$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
2ns(1-s)\ge ns \ge \sqrt{ns},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
while
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\sqrt{ns(1-s)} \ge \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{ns}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Therefore,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\min \left\{2ns(1-s),\sqrt{ns(1-s)}\right\}\ge \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{ns}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Consequently,
$$\mathbb E\left| \frac{\widetilde N}{n}- \frac{\widetilde M}{m} \right|\ge \frac{c_0}{n\sqrt{2}}\sqrt{ns}=c\sqrt{\frac{s}{n}},
$$
for a universal constant $c=\frac{c_0}{\sqrt{2}}>0$.

Set $N_p\coloneqq\max\left\{2^{8p},1024\right\}.$ We divide the proof into two cases. We first consider $n\ge N_p$ and use
the multiscale construction described in the main text. The remaining case
$1\le n<N_p$ is treated by a one-scale construction.

\textbf{Step 1: Multiscale construction for $n\ge N_p$.}
Assume that $n\ge N_p$ and set
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L\coloneqq\left\lfloor\frac{1}{4p}\log_2 n\right\rfloor.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $\frac{1}{4p}\log_2 n\ge2,$ we have

        {

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L\ge\frac{1}{8p}\log_2 n.

\gtrsim_p B\sqrt{\frac{\log(n+1)}{n}}

for every $2\le n<N_p$.

It remains to consider $n=1$. In this case, $N$ is a Bernoulli random
variable with parameter $1/2$. Conditioning on $N$ and applying Jensen's

\mathbb E\left|N-\frac{M}{m}\right|=\mathbb E\left[\mathbb E\left(\left|N-\frac{M}{m}\right|\middle|N\right)\right]\ge\mathbb E\left|
N-\mathbb E\left[\frac{M}{m}\middle|N\right]\right|.

\mathbb E\left|N-\frac{M}{m}\right|\ge\mathbb E\left|N-\frac12\right|= \frac{1}{2}.

\mathbb E\operatorname{W}_p^p(\widehat P_1,\widehat Q_m)\ge\frac{a^p}{2}=\frac{B}{\sqrt2}.

Hence,

Let $\widehat T = \widehat T(X_1,\ldots,X_n,Y_1,\ldots,Y_m)$ be an arbitrary estimator. 

Since $(\mu_0,\nu_0)$ and
$(\mu_\alpha,\nu_0)$ belong to $\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)\times\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)$ for every
$\alpha\in\mathcal A$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\sup_{\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)}\mathbb E_{\mu,\nu} \left|
\widehat T-\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu) \right|
&\ge \max\left\{ \mathbb E_{\mu_0,\nu_0}|\widehat T|,
\frac{1}{|\mathcal A|} \sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal A}
\mathbb E_{\mu_\alpha,\nu_0}
\left| \widehat T-\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_\alpha,\nu_0)\right|\right\}
\\
&= \max\left\{
\mathbb E_{\mathsf P_0\otimes\nu_0^{\otimes m}}
|\widehat T|, \mathbb E_{\mathsf P_1\otimes\nu_0^{\otimes m}}
\left| \widehat T-\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_{\alpha_\star},\nu_0) \right|
\right\},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the equality follows from the fact that
$\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_\alpha,\nu_0)$ has the same value for every
$\alpha\in\mathcal A$.

Applying the same two-point argument as in
Appendix~\ref{appendix:proofofeqlecam}, we obtain
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\sup_{\mu,\nu\in\mathcal K_{p,2p}(b)} \mathbb E_{\mu,\nu} \left| \widehat T -\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu,\nu)\right|
&\ge  \frac{\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_{\alpha_\star},\nu_0)}{4} \left[ 1- \operatorname{TV}\left( \mathsf P_0\otimes\nu_0^{\otimes m}, \mathsf P_1\otimes\nu_0^{\otimes m} \right) \right]
\\
&=
\frac{\operatorname{SW}_p^p(\mu_{\alpha_\star},\nu_0)}{4} \left[
1-\operatorname{TV}(\mathsf P_0,\mathsf P_1) \right].
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\end{align*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since the inequality holds for every estimator $\widehat T$, taking the
infimum over all estimators completes the proof.

We first verify that $\mu_0$ and $\mu_{\varepsilon}$ are probability measures. Since $0 \le \gamma_k \le \frac{\omega_k}{2}$ for all $k=1,\dots,L$, all the coefficients appearing in the definition of $\mu_0$ and $\mu_{\varepsilon}$ are nonnegative. Moreover, we also have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mu_0 \left(\mathbb{R}^d\right)=1-\Omega + \sum_{k=1}^L \omega_k=1,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
and, for every $\varepsilon \in \{-1,1\}^L$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\mathbb{R}^d\right)=1-\Omega+\sum_{k=1}^L\left(\frac{\omega_k}{2}+\varepsilon_k\gamma_k\right)+\sum_{k=1}^{L}\left(\frac{\omega_k}{2}-\varepsilon_k\gamma_k\right)=1.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Hence $\mu_0$ and $\mu_{\varepsilon}$ are probability measures.

We next compute their powered Sliced Wasserstein functional. For each $k$, both $\mu_0$ and $\mu_{\varepsilon}$ assign the same total mass $\omega_k$ to the pair $\left\{a_ke_1,b_ke_1\right\}$. Since these pairs are disjoint and ordered along the $e_1$-axis, the one-dimensional optimal coupling matches the mass in each pair with the mass in the same pair, so that no mass is transported between different pairs. Therefore, for any $\theta \in \mathbb{S}^{d-1}$,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{W}^p_p\left(\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\mu_{\varepsilon},\Pi^{\theta}_{\#}\mu_{0}\right)=\left|\langle e_1,\theta\rangle \right|^p\sum_{k=1}^{L}\gamma_k\left(b_k-a_k\right)^p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Therefore,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{SW}^p_p\left(\mu_{\varepsilon},\mu_0\right)=\left(\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\left|\langle e_1,\theta\rangle\right|^pd\sigma(\theta)\right)\sum_{k=1}^{L}\gamma_k\left(b_k-a_k\right)^p=\kappa_{p,d}\sum_{k=1}^{L}\gamma_k\left(b_k-a_k\right)^p.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    

Now, define the likelihood ratio
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L_n \coloneqq\frac{d\mathsf P_1} {d\mathsf P_0}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Applying Scheffé's theorem (see, for example \citet[Lemma~2.1]{tsybakov2008introduction}),
and Cauchy-Schwarz, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\operatorname{TV}\left(\mathsf P_0,\mathsf P_1\right)=\frac{1}{2} \mathbb{E}_{\mathsf P_0}\left|L_n-1\right| \le \frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf P_0}\left(L_n-1\right)^2\right]^{1/2}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf P_0}L^2_n-1\right]^{1/2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Therefore, it remains to derive an upper bound for the second moment $\mathbb E_{\mathsf P_0}L_n^2$. 

Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be independent random variables with common distribution $\mu_0$. For each $\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}^L$, define the one-sample likelihood ratio
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\ell _{\varepsilon}\coloneqq \frac{d\mu_{\varepsilon}}{d\mu_0}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $\mathsf P_1$ is the uniform mixture of the product measures $\mu_\varepsilon^{\otimes n}$, we have
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L_n\left(X_1,\dots,X_n\right)=2^{-L}\sum_{\varepsilon \in \{-1,1\}^L}\prod_{j=1}^n\ell_{\varepsilon}\left(X_j\right)=2^{-L}\sum_{\varepsilon \in \{-1,1\}^L}\prod_{j=1}^n\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\{X_j\}\right)}{\mu_0\left(\{X_j\}\right)}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Hence,
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
L^2_n\left(X_1,\dots,X_n\right)=2^{-2L}\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon'\in \{-1,1\}^L}\prod_{j=1}^n \frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\{X_j\}\right)\mu_{\varepsilon'}\left(\{X_j\}\right)}{\mu_0\left(\{X_j\}\right)^2}.
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
Since $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ are i.i.d. with common distribution $\mu_0$, taking expectations under $\mathsf P_0=\mu_0^{\otimes n}$ yields
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@align*} 
        {\begin{align*}}

    }

    } 
        {\csname pseudo@align*\endcsname[0]\relax}

&= 2^{-2L}\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon' \in \{-1,1\}^L}\left[\mathbb{E}_{X\sim \mu_0}\left(\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\{X\}\right)\mu_{\varepsilon'}\left(\{X\}\right)}{\mu_0\left(\{X\}\right)^2}\right)\right]^n\\
&=2^{-2L}\sum_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon' \in \{-1,1\}^L}\left[\sum_{z \in \text{supp} \left(\mu_0\right)}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}\left(\{z\}\right)\mu_{\varepsilon'}\left(\{z\}\right)}{\mu_0\left(\{z\}\right)}\right]^n.

4n\sum_{k=1}^L\varepsilon_k\varepsilon_k'\frac{\gamma_k^2}{\omega_k}\right)\right]\\

4n\varepsilon_k\varepsilon_k'\frac{\gamma_k^2}{\omega_k}\right)\right]

\\&\le\exp\left(8n^2\sum_{k=1}^L\frac{\gamma_k^4}{\omega_k^2}
\right),

& \le \kappa_{p,d}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{L}\omega_k\left(a^{2p}_k+b^{2p}_k\right)\right]^{1/2}\\
&\le \kappa_{p,d}\left(2\sum_{k=1}^{L} \omega_kb^{2p}_k\right)^{1/2}\\

<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{theorem:staudt}
Let $p \ge 1$, $p<q\le 2p$, $\epsilon>0$, and $1 \le n\le m$. Let $\eta_1$, $\eta_2 \in \mathcal{P}_{q+\epsilon}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)$, and let $\widehat{\eta}_{1,n}$ and $\widehat{\eta}_{2,m}$ denote the empirical measures associated with two independent samples from $\eta_1$ and $\eta_2$ respectively. 
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\begin{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[0]\relax} 
    
\mathbb{E}\left|\operatorname{W}^p_p\left(\widehat{\eta}_{1,n},\widehat{\eta}_{2,m}\right)-\operatorname{W}^p_p\left(\eta_1,\eta_2\right)\right|\lesssim n^{-1/2}+n^{-(q-p)/q},
 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        { 
    \@ifundefined{pseudo@equation*} 
        {\end{equation*}} 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    } 
        {\csname pseudo@equation*\endcsname[1]\relax} 
    
where the implicit constant depends on $p,q,\epsilon$ and the $(q+\epsilon)$- th moments of $\eta_1$ and $\eta_2$.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
